@@137710071 <h> Living Greener : Build a home for cheap ? Try using straw <p> YOUR home might be the costliest thing you will ever pay for ; ' mortgage ' comes from the French for ' death contract ' . <p> Home construction is also one of the most energy-intensive things we do in the modern world , consuming enormous resources and generating colossal amounts of waste . Programs like ' Grand Designs ' feature all manner of ' eco-friendly ' homes , but they tend to be only for the wealthy and hi-tech . <p> There are other ways , however , to build homes by hand using cheap materials . I have written here about building with cob , that mix of sand , clay and straw . <p> I have written about wattle-and-daub construction , weaving wicker walls and plastering over them . <p> And one 19th-century practice is also being rediscovered as a promising way to build cheaply -- straw . <p> Straw is plentiful ; Ireland alone could probably produce enough straw to meet most of its building needs , without importing any more materials @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ load-bearing walls or can simply insulate . It is lightweight and easy to transport to a building site , unlike steel or stone . <p> It is easy to work with , and -- if you use the old-fashioned , human-sized straw bales -- can be stacked and plastered by amateurs . Unlike most building materials , it contains no toxins that seep out over time . <p> Gathering and baling it does no damage to the environment , and the building waste can be made into mulch . The building material can be cut and shaped with a simple chainsaw or even a regular saw , allowing virtually any shape of house . <p> It is also one of the most perfect insulating materials around . Insulation is measured in ' R-values ' , and the higher the R-value , the less heat escapes the home . Most conventional homes are estimated to be R-12 to R-20 ; most bale homes , R-30 to R-50 . <p> The material has a natural trombe effect , allowing it to store heat or coolness and release it to keep your home @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ you ask ? Loose straw is , but bales are tightly compressed , and are no more flammable than wood . <p> The National Research Council of Canada , for example , found that a straw bale wall withstood temperatures of up to 1,850 degrees for two hours . <p> The big bad wolf can not blow the house down -- the Building Research Center of the University of New South Wales , Australia found in 1998 that bale walls withstood winds up to 134 miles per hour -- equivalent to a Category 3 hurricane . <p> In addition , they are beautiful . Since the walls are thick , they create deep niches for plant-boxes or window seats , and the plastered exteriors look rustic and natural but clean and satisfying . <p> Typically the house has a frame of wooden beams , and then straw bales are used to create the walls , and the straw plastered over to create an adobe effect . <p> Bales should not be used as load-bearing walls . It 's better to have a strong frame and let the bales form the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ or shift over time . <p> It also means that a wall damaged by moisture can be taken out and a new wall installed quite easily . <p> Americans in the Old West used this technique to build homes , barns , and even churches , and while the approach was abandoned for decades , builders have realized how sturdy , cheap and ecological most of these homes were . <p> Straw-bale homes are not perfect ; the walls must be kept free of moisture , so most such homes would need decks in rainy Ireland . <p> Pests and rodents might also be tempted to take up residence -- although that has been the case in most homes here , straw or not . <p> Most of all , it would be difficult to get planning permission to build this way these days , so most people who built this way did so secretly . <p> I visited a couple in rural Wales , two scientists who had worked on climate change for many years and wanted to build a sustainable community . <p> They and three other families @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ , most importantly , built four straw-bale homes quite quickly and easily -- all themselves , while never having worked on such a project before and with no professional building experience . <p> The homes cost them a miniscule amount compared to most homes , and as their insulation is so thick , their heating costs are also tiny . <p> Now , several years after their homes were built , they are all in great shape , and they had no expensive mortgages to pay off . <p> To find out if bale building is for you , consult books like ' Serious Straw Bale ' by Paul Lacinski and Michel Bergeron , or ' More Straw Bale Building ' by Chris Magwood.
